## *Chapter 5*  
**Gradient-based optimization approach to solve fuzzy algebraic equations governed engineering problems**

Paresh Kumar Panigrahi^([1](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_aff1)) and Sukanta Nayak^([2](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_aff2))

¹ Department of Mathematics, School of Engineering, Dayananda Sagar University, India

² Department of Mathematics, School of Advanced Sciences, VIT-AP University, India

## Abstract

In this chapter, we explore a gradient-based optimization technique to tackle a fuzzy-valued unconstrained optimization problem in which the objective function is fuzzy. In this regard, the fuzzy system of linear equation (FSLE) can be converted into fuzzy-valued optimization problem. Using the concept of fuzzy center and fuzzy arithmetic, the proposed approach can deal with the uncertain system. Then, using the proposed method, both only fuzzy (where either the coefficient matrix or right-hand side vector is fuzzy) and fully fuzzy (where both coefficient matrix and right-hand side vector are fuzzy) systems are investigated. Convergence analysis is conducted to ensure the existence of a solution, followed by solving a variety of example problems using the proposed method across different scenarios. Then, the obtained solutions are compared with the other existing methods, and it is found to be a good agreement.

**Keywords:** Fuzzy set; triangular fuzzy number; fuzzy optimization problem; fuzzy-valued function; fuzzy gradient-based optimization technique

## 5.1 Introduction

In mathematical modeling, the system of linear equations plays an important role in identifying field variables and reveals the hidden relationships between the involved parameters. Additionally, the presence of uncertainties in the system needs modification in mathematical models that yield an uncertain system of linear equations. In many physical problems, epistemic uncertainties are found very often. These uncertainties occur due to physical inaccuracy, mechanical defects, experimental errors, vague data, and impreciseness. As such, fuzzy set theory \[[1](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_1)\] can be used as a tool to handle the same. The inclusion of fuzzy makes it challenging to investigate the system using mathematical models. However, to study the system with greater precision, uncertainties cannot be avoided. In 1965, Zadeh introduced the concept of fuzzy sets \[[1](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_1)\]. Then, various researchers used fuzzy sets for their modeled problems. As the system of equations is an integral part of quantifying the modeled problems, researchers presented different approaches to solving a fuzzy linear system (FLS) equation. A few of the relevant research works are discussed here. In \[[2](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_2)–[5](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_5)\], authors presented the analytical method to solve fuzzy linear and quadratic equations. To find the solution of the linear system of the equations, variables and coefficients of a parameter are presumed to be either real or complex fuzzy numbers. In this context, Friedman *et al*. \[[5](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_5)\] used an idea to transform the fuzzy *n* × *n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_1.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> system into 2*n* × 2*n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_2.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> FSLE and then a simplified algorithm is presented to solve a FSLE. Recently, Abbasi and Allahviranloo \[[6](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_6)\] used the extension principle to solve FLS equations. These research works are carried out with an analytical approach.

However, due to the limitations of analytical approaches in the context of real-life problems, numerical methods can be adopted. In light of this, many relevant research works have been done with numerical methods to investigate the FSLE. Ma *et al*. \[[7](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_7)\] used an embedding method to solve the FLS equations with the concept of duality. Additionally, Dehghan and Hashemi \[[8](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_8)\] presented various iterative algorithms to solve the FSLE, whereas Dehghan *et al*. \[[9](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_9)\] employed an iterative algorithm to solve the fully FSLE. Further, the LU decomposition method is adopted by Abbasbandy *et al*. \[[10](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_10)\] to solve the FLS equations. Nayak and Chakraverty \[[11](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_11)\] considered the limit approach to defining fuzzy arithmetic and the same is adopted to solve the system. As intervals are the basis of fuzzy numbers, Karunakar and Chakraverty \[[12](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_12)\] introduced a technique to solve the interval system of equations. Besides, optimization techniques are helpful in the investigation of a system of linear equations. Hence, many contributions are reported to handle the system of equations under a fuzzy environment. Abbasbandy and Jafarian \[[13](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_13)\] have incorporated the steepest descent optimization method to solve the FLS equations. In addition, Nayak and Pooja \[[14](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_14)\] have investigated the interval system of the equation using an optimization method. Panigrahi and Nayak \[[15](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_15)\] proposed a fuzzy optimization technique to solve the system of equations. Panigrahi and Nayak \[[16](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_16)\] developed a derivative-free optimization technique to handle unconstrained optimization problems in a fuzzy environment. Furthermore, Panigrahi and Nayak \[[17](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_17)\] have applied the numerical method to solve the interval nonlinear system of equations.

The above literature review reveals that there are many analytical and numerical methods to solve the FLS equations. As such, the gradient-based optimization method can be used to solve the FLS equations. Furthermore, Abbasbandy and Jafarian \[[13](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_13)\] introduced the steepest descent optimization method to solve the FLS equations. Here, the authors investigate a 2*n* × 2*n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_3.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> system of linear equation. Therefore, the steepest descent gradient-based method is directly implemented for easy understanding to solve the same. One of the ideas is to use the parametric form for the FSLE to convert the crisp system. Then, the crisp system is computed by using the different cases. Besides this, the solution of this method can be discussed as the convergence theorem.

Therefore, we have extended the gradient descent optimization approach (GDOA) in a fuzzy environment. Here, we have used the proposed fuzzy gradient descent optimization (FGDO) to solve the linear system of equations for both only fuzzy and fully fuzzy by using different cases. The proposed gradient-based method works for the FSLE to give a better solution. To validate this method, convergence analysis is done here. Two example problems are studied, and the numerical solutions are discussed. Further, the solutions are shown graphically for easy interpretation. The solutions obtained are collected for three different cases and compared with two different approaches.

The chapter is organized as follows: [Section 5.2](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_5.2) provides an overview of fuzzy numbers and their arithmetic operations. [Section 5.3](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_5.3) categorizes FLS equations. [Section 5.4](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_5.4) introduces the gradient descent optimization method within a fuzzy framework, along with its convergence theorems. [Section 5.5](#ch05.xhtml#ch5_5.5) showcases the application of the FGDO in solving two example problems in a fuzzy environment, presenting the obtained solutions.

## 5.2 Preliminaries

This section includes the fundamentals of fuzzy numbers and its arithmetic \[[18](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_18)\].

A fuzzy number $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_4.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is defined as a convex normalized fuzzy set $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_5.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> on the real line ℝ<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_6.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> with the membership function

(5.1)$\\left. \\mu\_{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}}\\left( \\widetilde{x} \\right):\\mathbb{R}\\rightarrow\\left\\lbrack {0,1} \\right\\rbrack,\\ \\forall\\widetilde{x} \\in \\mathbb{R}, \\right.$

where $\\mu\_{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_7.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is piecewise continuous.

One of the fuzzy numbers, the triangular fuzzy number (TFN) is written as follows:

(5.2)$\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}} = \\left\\lbrack {\\widetilde{a}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R} \\right\\rbrack\\quad$

where *ã*[*L*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_8.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[*N*],<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_9.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *ã*[*R*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_10.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are the left, center, and right values of the TFN $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_11.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *ã*[*L*] ≤ *ã*[*N*] ≤ *ã*[*R*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_12.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

For example, if we take two TFNs, namely $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{L},\\ \\widetilde{a}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_13.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{B}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L},\\ \\widetilde{b}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_14.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, then they are said to be equal if  *ã*[*L*] = *b̃*[*L*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_15.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[*N*] = *b̃*[*N*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_16.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *ã*[*R*] = *b̃*[*R*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_17.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. The width of the TFN $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_18.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is defined as *w̃* = *ã*[*R*] – *ã*[*L*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_19.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

To compute TFNs, one may use the traditional arithmetic \[[19](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_19)\] operations. Let $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}\\ }\\mspace{-4mu} = \\left\\lbrack {\\widetilde{a}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_20.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> may be converted into an interval by using *α*− <img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_21.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>cut as shown below

$\\left. \\widetilde{\\mathcal{A}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R}} \\right\\rbrack = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{L} + \\left( \\widetilde{a} \\right.\_{N} - {\\widetilde{a}}\_{L}} \\right)\\alpha,\\ {\\widetilde{a}}\_{R} - \\left( {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R} - {\\widetilde{a}}\_{N}} \\right)\\alpha \\right\\rbrack,\\mathit{where}\\ \\alpha \\in \\left\\lbrack 0,1 \\right\\rbrack.$

Basically, the TFN is a collection of intervals, where each membership value *α* ∈ \[0, 1\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_22.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> corresponds to an interval. Consequently, when computing with TFNs, operations are conducted pointwise. Thus, it is necessary to construct a function incorporating the variable *α* to compute TFNs \[[20](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_20)\] effectively. In the next section, we will investigate into solving fuzzy systems of linear equations utilizing these arithmetic operations.

In the following, we may rewrite the definition of TFN \[[21](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_21)\].

A fuzzy number *x̃* = \[*x̃*[*L*],*x̃*[*N*],*x̃*[*R*]\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_23.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is classified as a TFN when its membership values are defined as follows:

(5.3)$\\mu\_{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{X}}}\\left( \\widetilde{x} \\right) = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {{\\widetilde{f}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L} \\leq \\widetilde{x} \\leq {\\widetilde{x}}\_{N}} \\\\ {{\\widetilde{f}}\_{R},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N} \\leq \\widetilde{x} \\leq {\\widetilde{x}}\_{R}} \\\\ {0,\\mspace{-22mu}\\mathit{otherwise}} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$

where, *f̃*[*L*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_24.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the left monotonically increasing function and *f̃*[*R*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_25.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the right monotonically decreasing function. These functions can be depicted as ${\\widetilde{f}}\_{L} = \\frac{\\widetilde{x} - {\\widetilde{x}}\_{L}}{{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N} - x\_{L}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_26.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and $f\_{R} = \\frac{{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R} - \\widetilde{x}}{{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R} - {\\widetilde{x}}\_{N}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_27.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

For computational convenience, an arbitrary TFN $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{X}} = \\left\\lbrack {\\widetilde{x}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_28.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. It can be converted into a two-variable form by transforming TFN to interval form and then interval to crisp form.

Using *α*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_29.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>\-cut, the TFN can be written as follows \[[22](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_22)\]:

(5.4)$\\widetilde{\\mathcal{X}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R}} \\right\\rbrack \\approx \\left\\lbrack {\\xi\_{L}^{\\alpha},\\xi\_{R}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack,$

where

*ξ*[*L*]^(*α*) = *x̃*[*L*] + *α*(*x̃*[*N*]−*x̃*[*L*]) *a**n**d* *ξ*[*R*]^(*α*) = *x̃*[*R*] + *α*(*x̃*[*N*]−*x̃*[*R*])*;* *α* ∈ \[0, 1\].

The crisp representation of TFN $\\widetilde{\\mathcal{X}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_30.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is obtained as follows:

(5.5)*ξ*[*L*]^(*α*) − *β*(*ξ*[*L*]^(*α*)−*ξ*[*R*]^(*α*)), *β* ∈ \[0,1\].

To investigate a fuzzy system, utilize the aforementioned form in the following systems.

## 5.3 Fuzzy linear system equation

In this section, we have discussed FLS, which can be classified into three different areas cases \[[23](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_23)\].

Consider a matrix form of the system of linear equations

(5.6)$\\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X} = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$

Here, *M̃*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_31.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> represents the coefficient matrix, *b̃*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_32.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> denotes the right-hand side vector, and *X̃*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_33.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> denotes the unknown vector to be determined. A brief presentation of this system is defined as

(5.7)$\\left. \\widetilde{M} = \\left\\lbrack \\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}} \\right.\_{\\mathit{ij}} \\right\\rbrack,\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}} = \\left\\lbrack {\\widetilde{b}}\_{1},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{2},\\ldots,{\\widetilde{b}}\_{n} \\right\\rbrack^{T},\\mspace{6mu}{and}\\mspace{6mu}\\widetilde{X} = \\left\\lbrack {\\widetilde{x}}\_{1},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{2},\\ldots{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n} \\right\\rbrack^{T},\\mspace{6mu} i,j = 1,2,\\ldots n$

Here, the entries *ã*[*i**j*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_34.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *b̃*[*j*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_35.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are consider TFNs within the system of equations with *n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_36.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> variables, that is,

(5.8)${\\widetilde{M}}\_{n \\times n}{\\mspace{6mu}\\widetilde{X}}\_{n \\times 1} = {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{n \\times 1},$

where

(5.9)${\\widetilde{M}}\_{n \\times n}\\quad = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{11} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{12} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{1n} \\\\ {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{21} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{22} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{2n} \\\\ \\vdots & \\vdots & \\ddots & \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{n1} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{n2} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{nn}} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},{\\widetilde{X}}\_{n \\times 1} = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{x}}\_{1} \\\\ {\\widetilde{x}}\_{2} \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{x}}\_{n} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\ {and}\\ {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{n \\times 1} = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{b}}\_{1} \\\\ {\\widetilde{b}}\_{2} \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{b}}\_{n} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}.$

The extended form of TFNs ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_37.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{j}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_38.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are

${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\mathcal{m}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{N\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}} \\right\\rbrack\\ {and}\\mspace{6mu}{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{j} = \\left\\lbrack {b\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}},b\_{N\_{\\mathit{ij}}},b\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}} \\right\\rbrack;i,j = 1,2,\\cdots,n.$

The fully fuzzy system of equation (FSE) can be defined as

${\\widetilde{M}}\_{n \\times n}\\quad = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{11} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{12} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{1n} \\\\ {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{21} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{22} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{2n} \\\\ \\vdots & \\vdots & \\ddots & \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{n1} & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{n2} & \\cdots & {\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{nn}} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\quad{\\widetilde{X}}\_{n \\times 1} = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{x}}\_{1} \\\\ {\\widetilde{x}}\_{2} \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{x}}\_{n} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\ {and}\\mspace{6mu}{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{n \\times 1} = \\begin{bmatrix} {\\widetilde{b}}\_{1} \\\\ {\\widetilde{b}}\_{2} \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ {\\widetilde{b}}\_{n} \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

where the entries of the coefficient matrix ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\mathcal{m}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{N\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_39.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and the right-hand side vector ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{j} = \\left\\lbrack {b\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}},b\_{N\_{\\mathit{ij}}},b\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_40.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are TFN. Here, the above system of equations is a *n* × *n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_41.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> FSE. Then, the subsequent system of equations is also *n* × *n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_42.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> system, which is based on the parametric form of TFNs. The next section includes the GDOA to solve the FSE.

## 5.4 FGDO method for FSLE

Here, the FGDO technique is adopted to solve the FSE and then followed by presenting a modified algorithm.

Consider a FSE $\\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X} = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_43.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, where *M*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_44.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is a symmetric positive definite matrix (SPDM). Then, we need to find the minimum of the quadratic function whose minimum is the solution of the system $\\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X} - \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_45.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Take the quadratic function

(5.10)$F\\left( X \\right) = \\frac{1}{2}{\\widetilde{X}}^{T}\\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X} - {\\widetilde{X}}^{T}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$

where 

$\\mspace{-18mu}\\widetilde{M} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{11}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{11}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{11}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{12}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{12}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{12}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\cdots & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{1n}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{1n}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{1n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{21}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{21}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{21}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{22}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{22}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{22}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\cdots & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{1n}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{1n}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{1n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\vdots & \\vdots & \\ddots & \\vdots \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{n1}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{n1}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{11}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{n2}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{n2}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{n2}}} \\right\\rbrack & \\cdots & \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{nn}}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{N\_{\\mathit{nn}}},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{nn}}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\ {\\widetilde{X}}\_{n \\times 1} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{1}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{1}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{1}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathit{x}}}\_{\\mathit{L}\_{2}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{2}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{2}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{n}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{n}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\end{bmatrix},\\ \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{1}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{1}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{1}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{2}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{2}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{2}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{n}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{n}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

The above-mentioned system is written as follows:

(5.11)$\\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{n}\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack.$

[Equation (5.11)](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.11) can be expressed equivalently as follows:

(5.12)$M\_{L} = \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{n}{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha} = {\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha}$

(5.13)$M\_{R} = \\sum\\limits\_{i,j = 1}^{n}{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha} = {\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}$

[Equations (5.12)](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.12) and [(5.13)](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.13) can be written as in quadratic function

$F\\left( X \\right) = \\left\\lbrack {M\_{L},M\_{R}} \\right\\rbrack = \\frac{1}{2}\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack^{T}\\left\\lbrack {{\\mathcal{m}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack - \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack^{T}\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack$

By using the gradient descent method for ([5.10](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.10)). We get the gradient of the given function as $\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_46.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is symmetric.

(5.14)$\\nabla F\\left( X \\right) = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack - \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack$

(5.15) − ∇*F*(*X*) = \[*b̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*b̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\] − \[*ã*[*L*[*i**j*]]^(*α*),*ã*[*R*[*i**j*]]^(*α*)\]\[*x̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*x̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\]

Let

(5.16)$\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{r}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{r}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack - \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k},$

be the residual point of *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_47.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. Then, *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_48.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is updated as follows:

(5.17)\[*x̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*x̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k* + 1] = \[*x̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*x̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*] − \[*λ̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*λ̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*]\[*r̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*r̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*]

and *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_49.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are updated by using the gradient descent approach, where the matrix *M̃*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_50.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is a nonsingular matrix.

\[*x̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*x̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k* + 1] = \[*x̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*x̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*] − \[*λ̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*λ̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*]\[*r̃*[*L*[*j*]]^(*α*),*r̃*[*R*[*j*]]^(*α*)\][*k*]

where

(5.18)$\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{\\lambda}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{\\lambda}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k} = \\frac{\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{r}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{r}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k}^{T}\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{r}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{r}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k}}{\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{r}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{r}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k}^{T}\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{a}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{r}}\_{L\_{j}}^{\\alpha},{\\widetilde{r}}\_{R\_{j}}^{\\alpha}} \\right\\rbrack\_{k}}.$

The above formulation will be further illustrated and solved through example problems in next section. Additionally, the algorithm will address the application of gradient-based optimization techniques and their extension in a fuzzy environment.

### 5.4.1 Fuzzy gradient descent algorithm

The above GDOA can be used in the following cases to solve the FSE ([Figure 5.1](#ch05.xhtml#fig5.1)).

\\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{1}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{1}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{1}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{2}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{2}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{2}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{L\_{n}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{N\_{n}},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{R\_{n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{1}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{1}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{1}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{2}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{2}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{2}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{b}}\_{L\_{n}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{N\_{n}},{\\widetilde{b}}\_{R\_{n}}} \\right\\rbrack \\\\ \\end{bmatrix}$

where the entries of the coefficient matrix ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{m}}}\_{\\mathit{ij}} = \\left\\lbrack {\\mathcal{m}}\_{L\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{N\_{\\mathit{ij}}},{\\mathcal{m}}\_{R\_{\\mathit{ij}}} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_57.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and the right-hand side vector ${\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}}\_{j} = \\left\\lbrack b\_{L\_{j}},b\_{N\_{j}},b\_{R\_{j}} \\right\\rbrack$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_58.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are TFNs.

The aforementioned cases will be explored and resolved through example problems. This algorithm is dedicated to addressing FSLE. To better understand the fuzzy gradient descent algorithm for FLS equations, we have discussed a few supporting theorems in the below paragraphs.

**Definition 1** For the gradient descent method using TFN, the field variables must possess convergence to the center and width of the obtained TFN.

As our system deals with fuzzy, we get a FSE. So, to solve the FSE, we have extended the gradient descent method for the FSE. The proposed gradient-based technique for TFN has been shown to be convergent. According to Definition 1, we must first demonstrate that the center value of TFN shows convergence and then establish that the width of TFN converges.

**Theorem 1** *If* *M*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_59.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> *is the real and SPDM, then solving* $\\widetilde{M}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_60.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> *is equivalent to minimizing the quadratic function* $f\\left( x\_{k} \\right) = \\frac{1}{2}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{M}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} - {\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_61.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

***Proof***

The quadratic function $f\\left( x\_{k} \\right) = \\frac{1}{2}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{M}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} - {\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_62.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>

Furthermore, the minimum of *f*(*X̃*[*k*])<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_63.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is obtained by taking the above FSDA

Considered an approximate solution *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_64.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

*x*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_65.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is updated by using the gradient descent method

(5.22)*X̃*[*k* + 1] = *X̃*[*k*] + *λ*[*k*]*r*[*k*]

The gradient of *f*(*X̃*[*k*])<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_66.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is

(5.23)∇*F*(*X̃*) = *M̃**X̃* − *b̃*

(5.24)$- \\nabla F\\left( \\widetilde{X} \\right) = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}} - \\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X}$

The negative gradient is the residual of the point *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_67.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, that is,

(5.25)$r\_{k} = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}} - \\widetilde{M}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}$

where *r*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_68.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the direction point of the *f*(*X*[*k*])<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_69.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Let

(5.26)*f*(*X̃*[*k* + 1]) = *f*(*X̃*[*k*]+*λ*[*k*]*r*[*k*])

$f\\left( x\_{k + 1} \\right) = \\frac{1}{2}\\left( {{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} + \\lambda\_{k}r\_{k}} \\right)^{T}\\widetilde{M}\\left( {{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} + \\lambda\_{k}r\_{k}} \\right) - \\left( {{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} + \\lambda\_{k}r\_{k}} \\right)^{T}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$

$\\frac{1}{2}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{M}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k} + \\frac{\\lambda^{2}}{2}r\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{M}r\_{k} + \\lambda\_{k}{\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{M}r\_{k} - {\\widetilde{X}}\_{k}^{T}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}} - \\lambda\_{k}r\_{k}\\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}.$

The function is minimum when *f*^′(*X̃*[*k*]) = 0<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_70.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> in terms of *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_71.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *r*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_72.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> by using ([5.16](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.16)) and ([5.17](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.17)),

$\\lambda\_{k} = \\frac{r\_{k}^{T}r\_{k}}{r\_{k}^{T}Ar\_{k}},\\mspace{6mu}{where}\\mspace{6mu}\\widetilde{X} = \\left\\lbrack {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{1},{\\widetilde{x}}\_{2},\\ldots,{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n}} \\right\\rbrack^{T}$

*X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_73.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> will be updated as a solution to the given system of linear equations.

Hence, *X̃*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_74.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> will converge in *n*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_75.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> number of iterations. It can be shown by the next theorem that the error decreases iteration-wise.

**Theorem 2** Suppose that *M*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_76.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is a positive definite matrix (PDM), and *w*[*k*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_77.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the error vector generated by the proposed algorithm and *η*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_78.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the eigenvalue of matrix *M*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_79.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, where *η*[max]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_80.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *η*[min]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_81.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the maximum and minimum eigenvalues. Then, by using the proposed algorithm of the *k**t**h*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_82.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> iteration satisfies the following condition:

(5.27)$\\left\\| w\_{k + 1} \\right\\|\_{\\widetilde{M}} \\leq \\frac{\\eta\_{\\max} - \\eta\_{\\min}}{\\eta\_{\\max} + \\eta\_{\\min}}\\left\\| w\_{k} \\right\\|\_{\\widetilde{M}}$

**Proof**

Let *M*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_83.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> be a positive definite matrix (PDM), then the error

*w*[*k*] = *x*^(\*) − *x*[*k*]

where *x*^(\*)<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_84.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> is the exact solution of $\\widetilde{M}\\widetilde{X} = \\widetilde{\\mathcal{Y}}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_85.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> produced by using the gradient descent approach.

Such that

$\\left\\| w\_{k + 1} \\right\\|\_{M} \\leq \\frac{\\eta\_{\\max} - \\eta\_{\\min}}{\\eta\_{\\max} + \\eta\_{\\min}}\\left\\| w\_{k} \\right\\|\_{M}$

and the gradient descent approach convergence to any initial gauss *x*[0]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_86.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>

∥*w*[*k*]∥[*A*] = *w*[*k*]^(*T*)*M**w*[*k*] > 0

where *M* is PDM.

$\\left\\| w\_{k + 1} \\right\\|\_{M} \\leq \\frac{\\eta\_{\\max} - \\eta\_{\\min}}{\\eta\_{\\max} + \\eta\_{\\min}}\\left\\| w\_{k} \\right\\|\_{M}$

$= \\frac{\\frac{\\eta\_{\\max}}{\\eta\_{\\min}} - 1}{\\frac{\\eta\_{\\max}}{\\eta\_{\\min}} + 1}\\left\\| w\_{k} \\right\\|\_{M}$

here the spectral condition number always $\\frac{\\left| \\lambda\_{\\max} \\right|}{\\left| \\lambda\_{\\min} \\right|} > 1$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_87.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, then after a few steps, *w*[*k* + 1]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_88.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> converges.

Hence, the convergence rate depends on the spectral condition number. If the spectral condition number is low, then the convergence rate is fast.

**Theorem 3** The gradient descent optimization method satisfies the given condition

(5.28)∥*x*^(\*)−*x*[*k* + 1]∥[2] < ∥*x*^(\*)−*x*[*k*]∥[2]

unless *x*[*k*] = *x*^(\*)<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_89.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>

Proof

The above statement can be proved by using the theorem given in \[[24](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_24)\].

Using Theorem 3, a unique solution of the linear system of equations can be obtained.

As the center solution of TFN means solution at *α*−<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_90.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>cut unity, Theorem 3 gives the center is convergence. Next, we need to prove the width of TFN. Theorem 3 shows the proof of the gradient descent method when the membership function is one. However, Theorem 4 is presented, when the membership function is zero.

**Theorem 4 \[15\]** Suppose {*x̃*[*n*]}<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_91.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and {*ỹ*[*n*]}<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_92.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are two convergent sequences for the left and right values of the TFN, respectively, such that $\\lim\\limits\_{n\\rightarrow\\infty}\\left. {\\widetilde{x}}\_{n}\\rightarrow\\widetilde{a} \\right.$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_93.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and $\\lim\\limits\_{n\\rightarrow\\infty}\\left. {\\widetilde{y}}\_{n}\\rightarrow\\widetilde{b} \\right.$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_94.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. Suppose, *η̃*[*n*]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_95.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *ξ̃*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_96.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> be the widths of the TFNs which are defined as *η̃*[*n*] = |*x̃*[*n*] − *ỹ*[*n*]|<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_97.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *ξ̃* = |*ã* − *b̃*|<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_98.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. Then, $\\lim\\limits\_{n\\rightarrow\\infty}\\left. {\\widetilde{\\eta}}\_{n}\\rightarrow \\right.\\widetilde{\\xi}$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_99.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Proof: Given, $\\lim\\limits\_{n\\rightarrow\\infty}\\left. {\\widetilde{x}}\_{n}\\rightarrow\\widetilde{a} \\right.$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_100.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and $\\lim\\limits\_{n\\rightarrow\\infty}\\left. {\\widetilde{y}}\_{n}\\rightarrow\\widetilde{b} \\right.$<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_101.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Let there exist *ϵ* > 0<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_102.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and integers *N*[1]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_103.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *N*[2]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_104.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> such that

$d\\left( {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n},\\widetilde{a}} \\right) < \\frac{\\epsilon}{2}\\mspace{6mu}\\mathit{for}\\mspace{6mu} n\_{1} > N\_{1}\\mspace{6mu}{and}\\mspace{6mu} d\\left( {{\\widetilde{y}}\_{n},\\widetilde{b}} \\right) < \\frac{\\epsilon}{2}\\mspace{6mu}\\mathit{for}\\mspace{6mu} n\_{2} > N\_{2}.$

Then, we need to prove that|*d*(*x̃*[*n*]−*ỹ*[*n*], *ã*−*b̃*)| < *ϵ*<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_313.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Using triangle inequality,

(5.29)*d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ỹ*[*n*]) ≤ *d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ã*) + *d*(*ỹ*[*n*],*b̃*) + *d*(*ã*,*b̃*).

[Equation (5.29)](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.29) gives

(5.30)$d\\left( {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n},{\\widetilde{y}}\_{n}} \\right) - d\\left( {\\widetilde{a},\\widetilde{b}} \\right) \\leq d\\left( {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n},\\widetilde{a}} \\right) + d\\left( {{\\widetilde{y}}\_{n},\\widetilde{b}} \\right) = \\frac{\\epsilon}{2} + \\frac{\\epsilon}{2} = \\epsilon.$

Similarly,

(5.31)*d*(*ã*,*b̃*) ≤ *d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ã*) + *d*(*ỹ*[*n*],*b̃*) + *d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ỹ*[*n*]).

[Equation (5.31)](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.31) can be represented as

(5.32)$d\\left( {\\widetilde{a},\\widetilde{b}} \\right) - d\\left( {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n},{\\widetilde{y}}\_{n}} \\right) \\leq d\\left( {{\\widetilde{x}}\_{n},\\widetilde{a}} \\right) + d\\left( {{\\widetilde{y}}\_{n},\\widetilde{b}} \\right) = \\frac{\\epsilon}{2} + \\frac{\\epsilon}{2} = \\epsilon.$

From ([5.30](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.30)) and ([5.32](#ch05.xhtml#equ5.32)), we get

(5.33)|*d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ỹ*[*n*])−*d*(*ã*,*b̃*)| ≤ *d*(*x̃*[*n*]−*ã*) + *d*(*ỹ*[*n*]−*b̃*) < *ϵ*∀*n* > max {*N*[1],*N*[2]}.

This proves

(5.34)|*d*(*x̃*[*n*],*ỹ*[*n*])−*d*(*ã*,*b̃*)| ≤ *ϵ*.

Therefore, we conclude that both the center and width of TFNs converge. The next section discussed two example problems to demonstrate the proposed algorithm and its effectiveness.

 

**Example problem** Here we discuss the proposed optimization approach to solve the FSLE using the following example problems.

 

**Example 1** Take the TFN linear system of equation \[[25](#ch05.xhtml#ref5_25)\]

*ã*[11]*x̃*[1] + *ã*[12]*x̃*[2] = *χ̃*[1]

(5.35)*ã*[21]*x̃*[1] + *ã*[22]*x̃*[2] = *χ̃*[2]

where *ã*[11] = \[2.5, 3, 3.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_105.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[12] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_106.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[21] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_107.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[22] = \[1.5, 2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_108.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[1] = \[0, 3, 5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_109.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[2] = \[1, 2, 7\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_110.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. Assume the initial approximation *x*⁰ = (0,0)^(*T*)<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_111.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, and tolerance value *ϵ* = 10^( − 3).<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_112.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>

**Case 1** Here, we consider the left-hand side elements that consist TFNs, i.e., the coefficient matrix is taken as fuzzy. Accordingly, the following coefficients are used for the investigation: *ã*[11] = \[2.5, 3, 3.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_113.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[12] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_114.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[21] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_115.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[22] = \[1.5, 2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_116.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> *χ̃*[1] = \[3\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_117.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[2] = \[2\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_118.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Applying the FGDO algorithm for an FSLE, the obtained solution vectors *x̃*[1]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_119.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, and *x̃*[2]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_120.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are listed in [Tables 5.1](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.1) and [5.2](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.2). The fuzzy solution is shown graphically in [Figure 5.2](#ch05.xhtml#fig5.2).

**Example 2** Take a FSLE

(5.36)$\\begin{matrix} {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{11}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{1} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{12}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{2} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{13}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{3} = {\\widetilde{\\chi}}\_{1}} \\\\ {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{21}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{1} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{22}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{2} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{23}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{3} = {\\widetilde{\\chi}}\_{2}} \\\\ {{\\widetilde{a}}\_{31}x\_{1} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{32}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{2} + {\\widetilde{a}}\_{33}{\\widetilde{x}}\_{3} = {\\widetilde{\\chi}}\_{3}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$

where *ã*[11] = \[3.5, 4, 4.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_185.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[12] = \[1.5, 2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_186.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[13] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_187.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[21] = \[1.5,2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_188.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[22] = \[6.5, 7, 7.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_189.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[23] = \[5.5, 6, 6.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_190.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[31] = \[−1.5, −1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_191.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[32] = \[5.5, 6, 6.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_192.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[33] = \[9.5, 10, 10.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_193.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[1] = \[−27,−20, −7\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_194.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[2] = \[1, 16, 40\],<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_195.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *χ̃*[3] = \[26, 44, 47\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_196.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>. Assume the initial approximation *x*⁰ = (0,0,0)^(*T*)<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_197.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, and tolerance value *ϵ* = 10^( − 3).<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_198.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>

**Case 1** Here, we consider the left-hand side elements that consist of TFNs, i.e., the coefficient matrix is taken as fuzzy. Accordingly, the following coefficients are used for the investigation: *ã*[11] = \[3.5, 4, 4.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_199.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[12] = \[1.5, 2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_200.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[13] = \[−1.5,−1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_201.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[21] = \[1.5, 2, 2.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_202.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[22] = \[6.5, 7, 7.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_203.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[23] = \[5.5, 6, 6.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_204.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[31] = \[−1.5,−1, −0.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_205.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[32] = \[5.5, 6, 6.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_206.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *ã*[33] = \[9.5, 10, 10.5\]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_207.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[1] =  − 20<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_208.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[2] = 16<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_209.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *χ̃*[3] = 44<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_210.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>.

Applying the modified gradient descent algorithm for FLS equations the obtained solution vectors *x̃*[1]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_211.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/>, *x̃*[2],<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_212.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> and *x̃*[3]<img src="../images/inline-math\_equ5\_213.gif" class="inline" alt="image"/> are listed in [Tables 5.8](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.8), [5.9](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.9), and [5.10](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.10). The obtained fuzzy solution is presented in [Figure 5.9](#ch05.xhtml#fig5.9).

**Case 3** In this case, both the left-hand and right-hand sides are treated as TFNs, i.e., both the coefficient matrix and the right-hand side vectors are considered fuzzy. From this study, it may be observed that in Case 1, the width of TFN convergence occurs at fewer iterations.

In the results and discussion, we highlighted the efficacy and significance of our proposed gradient-based optimization approach in solving fuzzy-valued optimization problems, particularly in addressing FLS equations. The proposed method demonstrates notable improvements in solution quality compared with existing approaches shown in [Table 5.5](#ch05.xhtml#tab5.5). Through rigorous convergence analysis, we validate the reliability and robustness of this method, supporting its suitability for tackling fuzzy systems. For instance, in the case of solely fuzzy systems, where either the coefficient matrix or the right-hand side vector is fuzzy, our method showcases superior convergence properties and solution gives a good agreement, which is shown in [Figure. 5.2](#ch05.xhtml#fig5.2)–[5.16](#ch05.xhtml#fig5.16). This is particularly evident when compared with traditional methods that may struggle to effectively handle uncertainty inherent in fuzzy systems. To provide concrete evidence of the present method's effectiveness, we present numerical solutions for two example problems, accompanied by graphical representations for intuitive interpretation. These results not only validate the applicability of our approach but also underscore its practical utility in real-world scenarios. In summary, the proposed gradient-based optimization approach represents a significant advancement in the field of fuzzy-valued optimization. Its ability to effectively handle uncertainty is a valuable tool for addressing complex fuzzy systems across various domains.

## 5.5 Conclusion

In this chapter, the GDOA is extended to address the FLS equations. To study the efficiency of the algorithm, a convergence theorem is presented. The GDOA is applied with two example problems of a system of linear equations in a fuzzy environment. The fuzzy system was divided into three cases, namely, only fuzzy (either the coefficient matrix or the right-hand side vector being fuzzy) and fully fuzzy system. Then, the same is solved through the proposed algorithm and the fuzzy solutions are reported. The obtained solutions are compared with other methods which found a good agreement. Finally, both the numerical and graphical solutions are discussed case-wise. The present study in different cases shows the uncertainty propagation and its nature for the three different cases. The study also suggest the sensitiveness of the system with respect to the place where uncertainty occurs.

The future scope of the present research in fuzzy optimization technique is promising and diverse. It includes integration with machine learning, multi-objective optimization, dynamic case, different types of fuzzy environment, and exploration of quantum computing. These directions offer opportunities for handling the uncertain field variable and addressing complex challenges across various domains.

## *Chapter 6*  
**A convolutional neural network-based biomarkers for Alzheimer's diagnosis and prognosis**

H. Meenal^([1](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_aff1)), C. Kishor Kumar Reddy^([2](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_aff2)), Reddaboina Rajini^([3](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_aff3)), Pendyala Loka Priya^([3](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_aff3)) and Marlia Mohd Hanafiah^([4](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_aff4))

¹ Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Methodist College of Engineering and Technology, Osmania University, India

² Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Stanley College of Engineering and Technology for Women, India

³ Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Keshav Memorial Institute of Technology, Jawaharlal Nehru Technological University, Hyderabad (JNTUH), India

⁴ Faculty of Science and Technology, University Kebangasaan Malaysia, Malaysia

## Abstract

Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are used by biomarkers to diagnose Alzheimer's disease (AD). To enable prompt intervention and improve patient outcomes, the project aims to improve early identification and precise prediction of disease development. With the help of Open Access Series of Imaging Studies (OASIS), the data is prepared for the model testing for prediction. The model train consists of a few steps like preprocessing including standardizing and augmenting imaging data. Functional and structural biomarkers suggestive of AD were trained into the CNN. Different metrics like accuracy, Roc curve is used for predicting model performance. To make sure the findings could be applied to a wider audience, cross-validation methods were used. When it came to differentiating between AD, MCI, and healthy control participants, the CNN model showed excellent accuracy. The model found distinct patterns of hippocampal shrinkage and alterations in cortical thickness as key biomarkers. By monitoring the course of the disease in MCI patients, the model's predictive abilities were confirmed and those who converted to AD were correctly predicted. The accuracy of CNN model is 99.95%. This work opens the door for the prediction of AD using the CNN model. The model's strong predictive abilities and high accuracy demonstrate its usefulness for early AD detection and monitoring in clinical settings. To further improve the model's therapeutic applicability, future studies should concentrate on integrating multimodal data and testing it across bigger and more diverse populations.

**Keywords:** Alzheimer's disease; biomarkers; prognosis; neuroimaging; early detection; precision medicine; neurodegenerative disorders; diagnostics cognitive decline

## 6.1 Introduction

Alzheimer's disease (AD) is basically a neurodegenerative disease that affects memory loss, behavior change, and lowering brain health overall \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1)\]. It is the most prevalent cause of dementia in the early stage and is distinguished by a steadily declining ability to think \[[2](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_2)\]. The illness progresses gradually, starting with mild memory loss and possibly leading to major impairment in day-to-day activities and fundamental physiological functions \[[3](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_3)\]. AD can cause a wide range of symptoms, but the most typical ones include memory loss, confusion, difficulty making decisions and solving problems, disorientation in time and space, and linguistic difficulties. People may find it difficult to remember recent discussions or occurrences in the early stages \[[4](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_4)\]. They may have behavioral and emotional changes as the illness worsens such as increased agitation, anxiety, or depression. Severe memory loss develops in later stages, accompanied by a reduction in cognitive function, an inability to recognize loved ones, and a decline in motor functions such as walking and swallowing.

AD is more common in older age groups; hence, elderly people are more vulnerable to it. Another important factor is genetics. People who have a family history of AD are more vulnerable \[[5](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_5)\]. Some genetic factors, such as the APOE ε4 allele, are linked to an increased risk of acquiring the illness.

Understanding these genetic predispositions is critical to identifying at-risk individuals and initiating early therapy \[[6](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_6)\]. AD risk also increases with other cardiovascular disorders such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol. This risk is further increased by lifestyle choices such as low cognitive engagement, poor eating habits, and physical inactivity. These elements emphasize the significance of preventing AD holistically, addressing lifestyle and genetic variables to slow the illness's start and progression. Through deep learning (DL)-based biomarkers, the prediction of Alzheimer's may be efficient and accurate, allowing for earlier intervention and better patient outcomes \[[7](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_7)\]. In order to create these markers, which will eventually help with AD early detection and therapy, it is critical to understand how genetic, cardiovascular, and lifestyle variables interact.

The data shows a steady trend wherein each year, there are more deaths among women than among men. 2010 saw 300,000 fatalities among women and 200,000 deaths among men. These numbers increased to 304,000 for males and 456,000 for women by 2023, continuing the long-term trend of women dying at a higher rate than men, graphically shown in [Figure 6.1](#ch06.xhtml#fig6.1).

Numerous techniques, including DL and machine learning (ML) techniques, are put forth. These are the Random Forest classifiers \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1)\], Decision Tree classifiers \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1)\], Support Vector Machine (SVM) \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1)\], Artificial Neural Network (ANN) \[[8](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_8)\], Generative Adversarial Network (GAN) \[[8](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_8)\], Stacked Auto-encoder (SAE) \[[3](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_3)\], Deep Neural Network (DNN), and Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) \[[4](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_4)\], Bidirectional encoder \[[4](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_4)\], Single Value Decomposition (SVD) \[[9](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_9)\], Principal Component Analysis(PCA) \[[5](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_5)\], Logistic regression \[[10](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_10)\], Stochastic Gradient Descent(SGD) \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\], Gradient Boosting Classifier (GBC) \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\], K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN) \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\], Multilayer Perception (MLP) \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\], PRISMA \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\], NeAE-Eye \[[12](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_12)\], Transfer Learning, and the Grad-CAM technique \[[7](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_7)\].

Existing approaches to AD diagnosis and prognosis face several significant limitations. The sensitivity and precision required by current approaches, including neuroimaging and biomarker assays, are sometimes lacking, especially in the early phases when treatments could have the greatest potential impact. The cost and discomfort of invasive treatments such as PET scans and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis prevent widespread usage. Subjective clinical evaluations can cause inconsistent diagnosis, which makes it more difficult to provide patients with consistent and trustworthy care. Furthermore, these methods might not be able to accurately anticipate the course of a disease or customize a course of therapy for a given patient, which emphasizes the need for more sophisticated and accurate diagnostic instruments. Integrating DL-based biomarkers could address these problems and enhance patient care and outcomes in AD by offering increased accuracy, non-invasiveness, and the potential for early diagnosis. By providing improved accuracy, non-invasiveness, and the possibility of early identification, by using DL-based biomarkers it is easy for the prediction of AD for the patient treatment at an early stage.

[Table 6.3](#ch06.xhtml#tab6.3) shows the analysis of several diagnostic procedures based on important factors such as cost, accuracy, simplicity of use, intrusiveness, and creative use of biomarkers.

Table 6.3 Comparison of diagnostic methods for Alzheimer's disease

| Diagnostic method || Invasiveness || Cost || Accuracy || Accessibility || Novel biomarkers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clinical assessments || Low || Low || Moderate || High || No |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Neuroimaging || Moderate || High || High || Moderate || Limited |
| CSF biomarkers || High || High || High || Low || Yes |
| Genetic testing || Low || Moderate || Moderate || High || No |
| Deep learning-based || Low || Low || High || High || Yes |

The Alzheimer's convolutional neural network (CNN) is the main topic of this chapter. The field of DL-based biomarkers, which have revolutionized determining AD diagnosis and prognosis, is led by CNNs. CNNs’ unparalleled ability to analyze and comprehend complex imaging data has allowed them to analyze and detect indications of AD through PET and MRI images from the dataset. Through automation of the feature extraction process, CNNs are able to detect minute but significant alterations to the brain's composition and capabilities that may occur before the clinical symptoms. This DL method increases diagnosis accuracy and aids in prognostic assessments by predicting the disease's trajectory. This chapter's primary focus is the Alzheimer's CNN. The field of DL-based biomarkers, which have revolutionized the diagnosis and prognosis of AD, is led by CNNs. Because of CNN decipher and evaluation of image data, it became easier to predict Alzheimer's using PET and MRI data. CNN is able to detect minute but significant alterations in the composition and functioning of the brain that take place prior to the clinical symptoms by the model extraction. By forecasting the course of the illness, this DL technique improves prognostic assessments and raises diagnosis accuracy. The chapter consists of [Section 6.2](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_6.2) about the Literature Survey followed by [Section 6.3](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_6.3) which is about the model used for Alzheimer's prediction while [Section 6.4](#ch06.xhtml#ch6_6.4) talks about the result and conclusion followed by Reference.

## 6.2 Literature survey

Koga *et al*. \[[1](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_1)\] used phosphorylated-tau (CP-13) pictures, CBD, and PSD databases. They suggested a model for AD that obtained performance values higher than 92% using decision tree and random forest classifiers. These results show the adaptability of the model for predicting AD diagnosis across various datasets. The Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) and the Open Access Series of Imaging Studies (OASIS) were two of the numerous datasets used by Arya *et al*. \[[2](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_2)\]. They proposed CNN, RNN, and ANN with the following accuracies: SVM 85.71%, CNN 98.6%, and RNN 91.2%. These results demonstrate the adaptability and potential of DL models for accurate AD diagnosis across a range of datasets. The ADNI and the OASIS were two of the few datasets used by Zhao *et al*. \[[8](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_8)\]. They suggested utilizing CNN, ANN, and radio frequency (RF), which produced a wide range of accuracy of 90.3%.

Many datasets like ADNI are used by Aqeel *et al*. \[[3](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_3)\]. They suggested employing DL techniques, which produced a wide range of results, including 88.2% accuracy, 88.64% precision rate, 88.39% F1-score, and 11.84% false negative rate. Error rate: 0.12% and area under the curve (AUC): 0.92%. These findings present the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on a variety of datasets. Abdelwahab *et al*. \[[13](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_13)\] utilized multiple datasets including microarray gene expression dataset. They proposed using Preprocessing Gene Selection (SVD, CNN, PCA) which resulted in an accuracy of 96.09% and an error rate of less than 1%. These findings highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on a variety of datasets. Numerous datasets like ADNI are used by Sellappan *et al*. \[[4](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_4)\]. They suggested a DL method known as Variational Bayesian Network, which produced results with an error rate of less than 1% and an accuracy of 98.57%. These findings highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD identification across many datasets. Magnetic Resonance Imaging and the OASIS datasets were among the most used datasets used by Mohammed *et al*. \[[9](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_9)\]. They proposed a classification approach that yielded results with an error rate of less than 6% and 94% accuracy rate, 93% precision, 98% recall, and 96% accuracy, along with AUC values of 94.8%, 93%, 97.75%, and 99.7%.

Hu *et al*. used datasets of ADNI \[[5](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_5)\]. They recommended DL techniques that produced a performance metric of 91.8% and an error rate of less than 9%. These findings highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise Alzheimer's on a variety of datasets. Odusami *et al*. \[[10](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_10)\] used datasets such as pet photographs from MRI and the ADNI. They recommended DL techniques, which produced results with a 73.90% accuracy rate and a 26.1% error rate. These findings highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on a variety of datasets. The MRI report is one of the datasets used by Dara *et al*. \[[6](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_6)\]. They recommended DL techniques, which produced precision: Genetic algorithms with SVM: 96.80 SVM with 93.30 image processing; Fisher: 96.32 SVM: 92.48, 95 Procedures for image processing and weight extraction; 91.40 Multimodal Neuroimaging arbitrary. Stochastic forest: 93 PCA using SVM: 95 CNN with various architectures: 98.08, less than 7.52% error rate on average. These findings show how versatile and promising DL models are for providing precise diagnoses of Alzheimer's across a variety of datasets. Syed *et al*. \[[14](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_14)\] employed datasets, including the Training cum-validation dataset and the independent test dataset. They suggested ML methods that yielded results with less than 1% error rate and 0.9799% accuracy.

Korean-based aging study for the diagnosis initiated the prediction of AD (KBASE; kbase.kr) dataset was one of the datasets used by Kim *et al*. \[[15](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_15)\] from the Severance Hospital. They recommended applying DL techniques; the results produced are accuracy with 78.05, sensitivity with 76.0, and F1-score with 74.0, respectively, with an error rate of 10–20%. These findings show how adaptable and efficient DL models are for predicting AD using datasets. The dataset OASIS was utilized by Bangyal *et al*. \[[16](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_16)\]. It was suggested to SVM, SGD, GBC, KNN, DT, RF, MLP. CNN provided accuracy of 94.61% for CNN and 92.12% for MLP with a 5.39 error rate range. The data collecting and preparation methods suggested by Sun *et al*. \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\] yielded an accuracy of 85% and an error rate of 15%. These outcomes highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on various datasets. Battineni *et al*. \[[17](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_17)\] used the OASIS dataset. CNN was suggested by them, and it produced results with an accuracy rate of 80% and an error rate of less than 20%. This shows the DL models' versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on various datasets. Agarwal *et al*. \[[18](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_18)\] used datasets from OASIS, ADNI, IXI, AIBL, MIRIAD, and MILAN. They advised using the PRISMA approach, which produced results with an error rate of less than 8.30% and an accuracy of 91.30%. Sun *et al*. \[[11](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_11)\] used eye tracking data. It was advised to use the NeAE-Eye approach, which produced results with an accuracy of 85% and an error rate of 15%.

Bringasa *et al*. \[[12](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_12)\] received information from the AFAC daycare center. They suggested using CNN, which produced results with an error rate of less than 9% and an accuracy of 90.91%. Raju *et al*. \[[7](#ch06.xhtml#ref6_7)\] employed two-dimensional coronal view MRI image slices that they obtained from Kaggle. They suggested using transfer learning in conjunction with the Grad-CAM approach, which produced results with a 1% error rate and 99% accuracy. These findings highlight the DL models’ versatility and promise for precise AD diagnosis on various datasets. [Table 6.4](#ch06.xhtml#tab6.4) shows the approaches for predicting AD using different datasets and models. These works leverage the MRI, ADNI, and OASIS datasets in addition to ML and DL methods, including CNN, RNN, SVM, and GAN. Performance evaluations demonstrate how well these techniques work to achieve high accuracy. Results are greatly enhanced by methods such as data augmentation and transfer learning.
